</p>
<div class="shortdescription nomobile noexcerpt noprint searchaux" style="display:none">King of France from 1226 to 1270</div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">"Louis IX" redirects here. For other uses, see <a href="/wiki/Louis_IX_(disambiguation)" class="mw-disambig" title="Louis IX (disambiguation)">Louis IX (disambiguation)</a>.</div>

</div></li></ul>
</div></td></tr><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header" style="background-color: #e4dcf6;line-height:normal;padding:0.2em 0.2em"><div style="height: 4px; width:100%;"></div></th></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Born</th><td class="infobox-data">25 April 1214<br /><a href="/wiki/Poissy" title="Poissy">Poissy</a>, France</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Died</th><td class="infobox-data">25 August 1270 (aged 56)<br /><a href="/wiki/Tunis" title="Tunis">Tunis</a>, North Africa</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Burial</th><td class="infobox-data"><div style="display:inline" class="label"><a href="/wiki/Basilica_of_St_Denis" class="mw-redirect" title="Basilica of St Denis">Basilica of St Denis</a></div></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Spouse</th><td class="infobox-data"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1151524712">.mw-parser-output .marriage-line-margin2px{line-height:0;margin-bottom:-2px}.mw-parser-output .marriage-line-margin3px{line-height:0;margin-bottom:-3px}.mw-parser-output .marriage-display-ws{display:inline;white-space:nowrap}</style>
<div class="marriage-display-ws"><div style="display:inline-block;line-height:normal;margin-top:1px;white-space:normal;"><a href="/wiki/Margaret_of_Provence" title="Margaret of Provence">Margaret of Provence</a></div>
   <div class="marriage-line-margin2px">&#8203;</div>&#32;<div style="display:inline-block;margin-bottom:1px;">&#8203;</div>&#40;<abbr title="married">m.</abbr>&#160;1234&#41;<wbr />&#8203;</div></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Issue_(genealogy)" title="Issue (genealogy)">Issue</a><br /><span style="font-weight:normal"><i><a href="#Children">among others...</a></i></span></th><td class="infobox-data"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1126788409"><div class="plainlist">

<li><a href="/wiki/Louis_of_France_(1244%E2%80%931260)" title="Louis of France (1244–1260)">Louis</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Philip_III,_King_of_France" class="mw-redirect" title="Philip III, King of France">Philip III, King of France</a></li>

</div></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Dynasty" title="Dynasty">House</a></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/House_of_Capet" title="House of Capet">Capet</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Father</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Louis_VIII,_King_of_France" class="mw-redirect" title="Louis VIII, King of France">Louis VIII, King of France</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Mother</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Blanche_of_Castile" title="Blanche of Castile">Blanche of Castile</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Religion</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholicism" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Catholicism">Roman Catholicism</a></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Louis IX</b> (25 April 1214 – 25 August 1270), commonly revered as <b>Saint Louis</b>, was <a href="/wiki/List_of_French_monarchs" title="List of French monarchs">King of France</a> from 1226 until his death in 1270. He is widely recognized as the most distinguished of the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Capet" title="House of Capet">Direct Capetians</a>. Following the death of his father, <a href="/wiki/Louis_VIII_of_France" title="Louis VIII of France">Louis VIII</a>, he was <a href="/wiki/Coronation_of_the_French_monarch" title="Coronation of the French monarch">crowned in Reims</a> at the age of 12. His mother, <a href="/wiki/Blanche_of_Castile" title="Blanche of Castile">Blanche of Castile</a>, effectively ruled the kingdom as <a href="/wiki/Regent" title="Regent">regent</a> until he came of age and continued to serve as his trusted adviser until her death. During his formative years, Blanche successfully confronted rebellious <a href="/wiki/Vassal" title="Vassal">vassals</a> and championed the Capetian cause in the <a href="/wiki/Albigensian_Crusade" title="Albigensian Crusade">Albigensian Crusade</a>, which had been ongoing for the past two decades.
</p><p>As an adult, Louis IX grappled with persistent conflicts involving some of the most influential nobles in his kingdom, including <a href="/wiki/Hugh_X_of_Lusignan" title="Hugh X of Lusignan">Hugh X of Lusignan</a> and <a href="/wiki/Peter_I,_Duke_of_Brittany" title="Peter I, Duke of Brittany">Peter of Dreux</a>. Concurrently, England's <a href="/wiki/Henry_III_of_England" title="Henry III of England">Henry III</a> sought to reclaim the <a href="/wiki/Angevin_Empire" title="Angevin Empire">Angevin continental holdings</a>, only to be decisively defeated at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Taillebourg" title="Battle of Taillebourg">Battle of Taillebourg</a>. Louis expanded his territory by annexing several provinces, including parts of <a href="/wiki/Aquitaine" title="Aquitaine">Aquitaine</a>, <a href="/wiki/Maine_(province)" title="Maine (province)">Maine</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Provence" title="Provence">Provence</a>. Keeping a promise he made while praying for recovery from a grave illness, Louis led the ill-fated <a href="/wiki/Seventh_Crusade" title="Seventh Crusade">Seventh</a> and <a href="/wiki/Eighth_Crusade" title="Eighth Crusade">Eighth Crusades</a> against the <a href="/wiki/List_of_Muslim_states_and_dynasties" title="List of Muslim states and dynasties">Muslim dynasties</a> that controlled <a href="/wiki/North_Africa" title="North Africa">North Africa</a>, <a href="/wiki/Egypt" title="Egypt">Egypt</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Holy_Land" title="Holy Land">the Holy Land</a>. He was captured and ransomed during the Seventh Crusade, and later succumbed to <a href="/wiki/Dysentery" title="Dysentery">dysentery</a> during the Eighth Crusade. His son, <a href="/wiki/Philip_III_of_France" title="Philip III of France">Philip III</a>, succeeded him.
</p><p>Louis instigated significant reforms in the <a href="/wiki/Law_of_France" title="Law of France">French legal system</a>, creating a <a href="/wiki/Royal_justice" title="Royal justice">royal justice</a> mechanism that allowed petitioners to <a href="/wiki/Appeal" title="Appeal">appeal</a> judgements directly to the monarch. He abolished <a href="/wiki/Trial_by_ordeal" title="Trial by ordeal">trials by ordeal</a>, endeavored to terminate private wars, and incorporated the <a href="/wiki/Presumption_of_innocence" title="Presumption of innocence">presumption of innocence</a> into <a href="/wiki/Criminal_procedure" title="Criminal procedure">criminal proceedings</a>. To implement his new legal framework, he established the offices of <a href="/wiki/Provost_(military_police)" title="Provost (military police)">provosts</a> and <a href="/wiki/Bailiff_(France)" title="Bailiff (France)">bailiffs</a>. Louis IX's reign is often marked as an economic and political zenith for <a href="/wiki/France_in_the_Middle_Ages" title="France in the Middle Ages">medieval France</a>, and he held immense respect throughout <a href="/wiki/Christendom" title="Christendom">Christendom</a>. His reputation as a fair and judicious ruler led to his being solicited to mediate disputes beyond his own kingdom.<sup id="cite_ref-goyau_1-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-goyau-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Louis_IX_&#123;&#123;!&#125;&#125;_king_of_France_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Louis_IX_{{!}}_king_of_France-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Louis' admirers through the centuries have celebrated him as the quintessential Christian monarch. His skill as a knight and engaging manner with the public contributed to his popularity, although he was occasionally criticized as being overly pious, earning the moniker of a "<a href="/wiki/Monk" title="Monk">monk</a> king".<sup id="cite_ref-Louis_IX_&#123;&#123;!&#125;&#125;_king_of_France_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Louis_IX_{{!}}_king_of_France-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> Despite his progressive legal reforms, Louis was a staunch Christian and rigorously enforced <a href="/wiki/Catholic_theology" title="Catholic theology">Catholic orthodoxy</a>. He enacted harsh laws against <a href="/wiki/Blasphemy" title="Blasphemy">blasphemy</a><sup id="cite_ref-Bobineau_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bobineau-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> and launched actions against <a href="/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_France" title="History of the Jews in France">France's Jewish population</a>, including the notorious burning of the <a href="/wiki/Talmud" title="Talmud">Talmud</a> following the <a href="/wiki/Disputation_of_Paris" title="Disputation of Paris">Disputation of Paris</a>. Louis IX holds the unique distinction of being the sole <a href="/wiki/Canonization" title="Canonization">canonized</a> king of France.<sup id="cite_ref-The_Pope_Who_Saved_the_Talmud_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-The_Pope_Who_Saved_the_Talmud-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Sources">Sources</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_IX_of_France&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Sources">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Much of what is known of Louis's life comes from <a href="/wiki/Jean_de_Joinville" title="Jean de Joinville">Jean de Joinville</a>'s famous <i><a href="/wiki/Jean_de_Joinville#Life_of_Saint_Louis" title="Jean de Joinville">Life of Saint Louis</a></i>. Joinville was a close friend, confidant, and counselor to the king. He participated as a witness in the papal inquest into Louis's life that resulted in his canonization in 1297 by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Boniface_VIII" title="Pope Boniface VIII">Pope Boniface VIII</a>.
</p><p>Two other important biographies were written by the king's confessor, <a href="/wiki/Geoffrey_of_Beaulieu" title="Geoffrey of Beaulieu">Geoffrey of Beaulieu</a>, and his chaplain, <a href="/wiki/William_of_Chartres_(Dominican)" title="William of Chartres (Dominican)">William of Chartres</a>. While several individuals wrote biographies in the decades following the king's death, only Jean of Joinville, Geoffrey of Beaulieu, and William of Chartres wrote from personal knowledge of the king and of the events they describe, and all three are biased favorably to the king. The fourth important source of information is William of Saint-Parthus's 19th-century biography,<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> which he wrote using material from the papal inquest mentioned above.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_IX_of_France&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Louis was born on 25 April 1214 at <a href="/wiki/Poissy" title="Poissy">Poissy</a>, near Paris, the son of <a href="/wiki/Louis_VIII_of_France" title="Louis VIII of France">Louis the Lion</a> and <a href="/wiki/Blanche_of_Castile" title="Blanche of Castile">Blanche of Castile</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERichard1983xxiv_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERichard1983xxiv-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> and was baptized there in <i>La Collégiale Notre-Dame</i> church. His grandfather on his father's side was <a href="/wiki/Philip_II_of_France" title="Philip II of France">Philip II</a>, king of France; his grandfather on his mother's side was <a href="/wiki/Alfonso_VIII_of_Castile" title="Alfonso VIII of Castile">Alfonso VIII</a>, king of <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Castile" title="Kingdom of Castile">Castile</a>. <a href="/wiki/Tutors" class="mw-redirect" title="Tutors">Tutors</a> of Blanche's choosing taught him <a href="/wiki/Latin" title="Latin">Latin</a>, public speaking, writing, military arts, and government.<sup id="cite_ref-archstl_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-archstl-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> He was nine years old when his grandfather Philip II died and his father became King Louis VIII.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Minority_.281226.E2.80.931234.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Minority_(1226–1234)">Minority (1226–1234)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_IX_of_France&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Minority (1226–1234)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Louis was 12 years old when his father died on 8 November 1226. He was crowned king within the month at <a href="/wiki/Reims_Cathedral" title="Reims Cathedral">Reims Cathedral</a>. His mother, Blanche, ruled France as <a href="/wiki/Regent" title="Regent">regent</a> during his minority.<sup id="cite_ref-Encarta-Louis_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Encarta-Louis-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> Louis's mother instilled in him her devout Christianity. She is once recorded to have said:<sup id="cite_ref-SHMI_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SHMI-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>I love you, my dear son, as much as a mother can love her child; but I would rather see you dead at my feet than that you should ever commit a mortal sin.</p></blockquote>
<p>His younger brother <a href="/wiki/Charles_I_of_Sicily" class="mw-redirect" title="Charles I of Sicily">Charles I of Sicily</a> (1227–85) was created <a href="/wiki/Count_of_Anjou" class="mw-redirect" title="Count of Anjou">count of Anjou</a>, thus founding the <a href="/wiki/Capetian_House_of_Anjou" title="Capetian House of Anjou">Capetian Angevin</a> dynasty.
</p><p>In 1229, when Louis was 15, his mother ended the <a href="/wiki/Albigensian_Crusade" title="Albigensian Crusade">Albigensian Crusade</a> by signing an agreement with <a href="/wiki/Raymond_VII,_Count_of_Toulouse" title="Raymond VII, Count of Toulouse">Raymond VII of Toulouse</a>. <a href="/wiki/Raymond_VI,_Count_of_Toulouse" title="Raymond VI, Count of Toulouse">Raymond VI of Toulouse</a> had been suspected of ordering the assassination of <a href="/wiki/Pierre_de_Castelnau" title="Pierre de Castelnau">Pierre de Castelnau</a>, a Roman Catholic preacher who attempted to convert the <a href="/wiki/Cathars" class="mw-redirect" title="Cathars">Cathars</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESumption197815_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESumption197815-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 27 May 1234, Louis married <a href="/wiki/Margaret_of_Provence" title="Margaret of Provence">Margaret of Provence</a> (1221–1295); she was crowned in the cathedral of Sens the next day.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERichard198364_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERichard198364-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> Margaret was the sister of <a href="/wiki/Eleanor_of_Provence" title="Eleanor of Provence">Eleanor of Provence</a>, who later married <a href="/wiki/Henry_III_of_England" title="Henry III of England">Henry III of England</a>.  The new queen's religious zeal made her a well-suited partner for the king, and they are attested to have gotten along well, enjoying riding together, reading, and listening to music. His closeness to Margaret aroused jealousy in his mother, who tried to keep the couple apart as much as she could.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERichard198365_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERichard198365-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>While his contemporaries viewed his reign as co-rule between the king and his mother, historians generally believe Louis began ruling personally in 1234, with his mother assuming a more advisory role.<sup id="cite_ref-goyau_1-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-goyau-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> She continued to have a strong influence on the king until her death in 1252.<sup id="cite_ref-Encarta-Louis_10-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Encarta-Louis-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEShadis201017–19_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEShadis201017–19-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Louis_as_king">Louis as king</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_IX_of_France&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Louis as king">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Arts">Arts</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_IX_of_France&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Arts">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Louis-innocentiv.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/d/df/Louis-innocentiv.jpg/220px-Louis-innocentiv.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="270" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/d/df/Louis-innocentiv.jpg/330px-Louis-innocentiv.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/d/df/Louis-innocentiv.jpg 2x" data-file-width="344" data-file-height="422" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Pope_Innocent_IV" title="Pope Innocent IV">Pope Innocent IV</a> with Louis IX at <a href="/wiki/Cluny" title="Cluny">Cluny</a></figcaption></figure><p>Louis's patronage of the arts inspired much innovation in <a href="/wiki/Gothic_art" title="Gothic art">Gothic art</a> and <a href="/wiki/Gothic_architecture" title="Gothic architecture">architecture</a>. The style of his court was influential throughout Europe, both because of artwork purchased from Parisian masters for export, and by the marriage of the king's daughters and other female relatives to foreigners. They became emissaries of Parisian models and styles elsewhere. Louis's personal chapel, the <i><a href="/wiki/Sainte-Chapelle" title="Sainte-Chapelle">Sainte-Chapelle</a></i> in Paris, which was known for its intricate stained-glass windows, was copied more than once by his descendants elsewhere. Louis is believed to have ordered the production of the <a href="/wiki/Morgan_Bible" title="Morgan Bible">Morgan Bible</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Arsenal_Bible" class="mw-redirect" title="Arsenal Bible">Arsenal Bible</a>, both deluxe <a href="/wiki/Illuminated_manuscript" title="Illuminated manuscript">illuminated manuscripts</a>.
</p><p>During the so-called "golden century of Saint Louis", the kingdom of France was at its height in Europe, both politically and economically. Saint Louis was regarded as <i>"<a href="/wiki/Primus_inter_pares" title="Primus inter pares">primus inter pares</a>",</i> first among equals, among the kings and rulers of the continent. He commanded the largest army and ruled the largest and wealthiest kingdom, the European centre of arts and intellectual thought at the time. The foundations for the notable college of theology, later known as the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Paris" title="University of Paris">Sorbonne</a>, were laid in Paris about the year 1257.<sup id="cite_ref-crawley_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-crawley-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Arbitration">Arbitration</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_IX_of_France&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Arbitration">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The prestige and respect felt by Europeans for King Louis IX were due more to the appeal of his personality than to military domination. For his contemporaries, he was the quintessential example of the Christian prince and embodied the whole of <a href="/wiki/Christendom" title="Christendom">Christendom</a> in his person. His reputation for fairness and even saintliness was already well established while he was alive, and on many occasions he was chosen as an arbiter in quarrels among the rulers of Europe.<sup id="cite_ref-goyau_1-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-goyau-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Shortly before 1256, <a href="/wiki/Enguerrand_IV,_Lord_of_Coucy" class="mw-redirect" title="Enguerrand IV, Lord of Coucy">Enguerrand IV, Lord of Coucy</a>, arrested and without trial hanged three young squires of Laon, whom he accused of poaching in his forest. In 1256 Louis had the lord arrested and brought to the <a href="/wiki/Palais_du_Louvre" class="mw-redirect" title="Palais du Louvre">Louvre</a> by his sergeants. Enguerrand demanded judgment by his peers and trial by battle, which the king refused because he thought it obsolete. Enguerrand was tried, sentenced, and ordered to pay 12,000 livres. Part of the money was to pay for <a href="/wiki/Mass_(liturgy)" title="Mass (liturgy)">masses</a> to be said in perpetuity for the souls of the men he had hanged.
</p><p>In 1258, Louis and <a href="/wiki/James_I_of_Aragon" title="James I of Aragon">James I of Aragon</a> signed the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Corbeil_(1258)" title="Treaty of Corbeil (1258)">Treaty of Corbeil</a> to end areas of contention between them. By this treaty, Louis renounced his feudal overlordship over the <a href="/wiki/County_of_Barcelona" title="County of Barcelona">County of Barcelona</a> and <a href="/wiki/Roussillon" title="Roussillon">Roussillon</a>, which was held by the King of Aragon. James in turn renounced his feudal overlordship over several counties in southern France, including <a href="/wiki/Provence" title="Provence">Provence</a> and <a href="/wiki/Languedoc" title="Languedoc">Languedoc</a>. In 1259 Louis signed the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_(1259)" title="Treaty of Paris (1259)">Treaty of Paris</a>, by which <a href="/wiki/Henry_III_of_England" title="Henry III of England">Henry III of England</a> was confirmed in his possession of territories in southwestern France, and Louis received the provinces of <a href="/wiki/County_of_Anjou" title="County of Anjou">Anjou</a>, <a href="/wiki/Normandy" title="Normandy">Normandy</a> (Normandie), <a href="/wiki/Poitou" title="Poitou">Poitou</a>, <a href="/wiki/Maine_(province)" title="Maine (province)">Maine</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Touraine" title="Touraine">Touraine</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Encarta-Louis_10-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Encarta-Louis-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Religion">Religion</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_IX_of_France&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Religion">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>

<div class="sidebar-list mw-collapsible mw-collapsed hlist"><div class="sidebar-list-title" style="border-top:1px solid #cc0000;background:transparent;text-align:center;">Organizations</div><div class="sidebar-list-content mw-collapsible-content">
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Action_Fran%C3%A7aise" title="Action Française">Action Française</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Acci%C3%B3n_Espa%C3%B1ola" title="Acción Española">Acción Española</a></li>

</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-content">

</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-below plainlist" style="font-weight:normal; border-top:1px solid #CC0000; border-bottom:1px solid #CC0000;">
<ul><li><span class="mw-image-border noviewer" typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:A_coloured_voting_box.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="icon" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/0/01/A_coloured_voting_box.svg/16px-A_coloured_voting_box.svg.png" decoding="async" width="16" height="16" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/0/01/A_coloured_voting_box.svg/24px-A_coloured_voting_box.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/0/01/A_coloured_voting_box.svg/32px-A_coloured_voting_box.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="160" data-file-height="160" /></a></span>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Portal:Politics" title="Portal:Politics">Politics&#32;portal</a></li></ul></td></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::after{margin-left:-0.125em;content:" ]"}.mw-parser-output .navbar li{word-spacing:-0.125em}.mw-parser-output .navbar a>span,.mw-parser-output .navbar a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-full{font-size:114%;margin:0 7em}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Integralism" title="Template:Integralism"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Integralism" title="Template talk:Integralism"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Integralism&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table><p>
The perception of Louis IX by his contemporaries as the exemplary Christian prince was reinforced by his religious zeal. Louis was an extremely devout Catholic, and he built the <i><a href="/wiki/Sainte-Chapelle" title="Sainte-Chapelle">Sainte-Chapelle</a></i> ("Holy Chapel"),<sup id="cite_ref-goyau_1-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-goyau-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> located within the royal palace complex (now the <a href="/wiki/Paris_Hall_of_Justice" class="mw-redirect" title="Paris Hall of Justice">Paris Hall of Justice</a>), on the <i><a href="/wiki/%C3%8Ele_de_la_Cit%C3%A9" title="Île de la Cité">Île de la Cité</a></i> in the centre of Paris. The <i>Sainte Chapelle</i>, a prime example of the <a href="/wiki/Rayonnant" title="Rayonnant">Rayonnant</a> style of <a href="/wiki/Gothic_architecture" title="Gothic architecture">Gothic architecture</a>, was erected as a shrine for what Louis believed to be the <a href="/wiki/Crown_of_Thorns" class="mw-redirect" title="Crown of Thorns">Crown of Thorns</a> and a fragment of the <a href="/wiki/True_Cross" title="True Cross">True Cross</a>, supposed precious <a href="/wiki/Relic" title="Relic">relics</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Passion_(Christianity)" class="mw-redirect" title="Passion (Christianity)">Passion</a> of Christ. He acquired these in 1239–41 from Emperor <a href="/wiki/Baldwin_II,_Latin_Emperor" title="Baldwin II, Latin Emperor">Baldwin II</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Latin_Empire" title="Latin Empire">Latin Empire</a> of <a href="/wiki/Constantinople" title="Constantinople">Constantinople</a> by agreeing to pay off Baldwin's debt to the Venetian merchant Niccolo Quirino, for which Baldwin had pledged the Crown of Thorns as collateral.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> Louis IX paid the exorbitant sum of 135,000 <a href="/wiki/French_livre" title="French livre">livres</a> to clear the debt.</p><figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:San_Domenico47.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/de/San_Domenico47.jpg/220px-San_Domenico47.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="225" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/de/San_Domenico47.jpg/330px-San_Domenico47.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/de/San_Domenico47.jpg/440px-San_Domenico47.jpg 2x" data-file-width="898" data-file-height="920" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Reliquary" title="Reliquary">Reliquary</a> of Saint Louis (end of the 13th century) <a href="/wiki/Basilica_of_Saint_Dominic" class="mw-redirect" title="Basilica of Saint Dominic">Basilica of Saint Dominic</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bologna" title="Bologna">Bologna</a>, Italy</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1230, the King forbade all forms of <a href="/wiki/Usury" title="Usury">usury</a>, defined at the time as any taking of interest and therefore covering most banking activities. Louis used these anti-usury laws to extract funds from Jewish and Lombard moneylenders, with the hopes that it would help pay for a future crusade.<sup id="cite_ref-crawley_16-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-crawley-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> Louis also oversaw the <a href="/wiki/Disputation_of_Paris" title="Disputation of Paris">Disputation of Paris</a> in 1240, in which Paris's Jewish leaders were imprisoned and forced to admit to anti-Christian passages in the Talmud, the major source of Jewish commentaries on the Bible and religious law. As a result of the disputation, Pope <a href="/wiki/Pope_Gregory_IX" title="Pope Gregory IX">Gregory IX</a> declared that all copies of the Talmud should be seized and destroyed. In 1242, Louis ordered the burning of 12,000 Talmudim, along with other important Jewish books and scripture.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> The edict against the Talmud was eventually overturned by Gregory IX's successor, <a href="/wiki/Pope_Innocent_IV" title="Pope Innocent IV">Innocent IV</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-The_Pope_Who_Saved_the_Talmud_5-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-The_Pope_Who_Saved_the_Talmud-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Louis also expanded the scope of the <a href="/wiki/Inquisition" title="Inquisition">Inquisition</a> in France. He set the punishment for <a href="/wiki/Blasphemy" title="Blasphemy">blasphemy</a> to <a href="/wiki/Mutilation" title="Mutilation">mutilation</a> of the tongue and lips.<sup id="cite_ref-Bobineau_4-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bobineau-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> The area most affected by this expansion was southern France, where the <a href="/wiki/Cathar" class="mw-redirect" title="Cathar">Cathar</a> sect had been strongest. The rate of confiscation of property from the Cathars and others reached its highest levels in the years before his first crusade and slowed upon his return to France in 1254.
</p><p>In 1250, Louis headed a crusade to Egypt and was taken prisoner. During his captivity, he recited the <a href="/wiki/Canonical_hours" title="Canonical hours">Divine Office</a> every day. After his release against ransom, he visited the Holy Land before returning to France.<sup id="cite_ref-SHMI_11-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SHMI-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> In these deeds, Louis IX tried to fulfill what he considered the duty of France as "the eldest daughter of the Church" (<i>la fille aînée de l'Église</i>), a tradition of protector of the Church going back to the <a href="/wiki/Franks" title="Franks">Franks</a> and <a href="/wiki/Charlemagne" title="Charlemagne">Charlemagne</a>, who had been crowned by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Leo_III" title="Pope Leo III">Pope Leo III</a> in <a href="/wiki/Rome" title="Rome">Rome</a> in 800. The kings of France were known in the Church by the title "most Christian king" (<i>Rex Christianissimus</i>).
</p><p>Louis founded many hospitals and houses: the House of the <a href="/wiki/Filles-Dieu" title="Filles-Dieu">Filles-Dieu</a> for reformed prostitutes; the <a href="/wiki/Quinze-Vingts_National_Eye_Hospital" class="mw-redirect" title="Quinze-Vingts National Eye Hospital">Quinze-Vingt</a> for 300 blind men (1254), and hospitals at Pontoise, Vernon, and Compiégne.<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>St. Louis installed a house of the <a href="/wiki/Trinitarian_Order" class="mw-redirect" title="Trinitarian Order">Trinitarian Order</a> at <a href="/wiki/Palace_of_Fontainebleau" title="Palace of Fontainebleau">Fontainebleau</a>, his chateau and estate near Paris. He chose Trinitarians as his chaplains and was accompanied by them on his crusades. In his spiritual testament he wrote, "My dearest son, you should permit yourself to be tormented by every kind of martyrdom before you would allow yourself to commit a mortal sin."<sup id="cite_ref-SHMI_11-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SHMI-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div style="clear:left;"></div>
<p>Louis authored and sent the <i>Enseignements</i>, or teachings, to his son <a href="/wiki/Philip_III_of_France" title="Philip III of France">Philip III</a>. The letter outlined how Philip should follow the example of Jesus Christ in order to be a moral leader.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> The letter is estimated to have been written in 1267, three years before Louis's death.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Personal_reign_.281235.E2.80.931266.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Personal_reign_(1235–1266)">Personal reign (1235–1266)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_IX_of_France&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Personal reign (1235–1266)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Seventh_Crusade">Seventh Crusade</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_IX_of_France&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Seventh Crusade">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:C_croisade7_prisonnier1.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c6/C_croisade7_prisonnier1.jpg/170px-C_croisade7_prisonnier1.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="220" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c6/C_croisade7_prisonnier1.jpg/255px-C_croisade7_prisonnier1.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c6/C_croisade7_prisonnier1.jpg/340px-C_croisade7_prisonnier1.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2183" data-file-height="2827" /></a><figcaption>Louis IX was taken prisoner at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Fariskur_(1250)" title="Battle of Fariskur (1250)">Battle of Fariskur</a>, during the Seventh Crusade (<a href="/wiki/Gustave_Dor%C3%A9" title="Gustave Doré">Gustave Doré</a>).</figcaption></figure>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Seventh_Crusade" title="Seventh Crusade">Seventh Crusade</a></div>
<p>Louis and his followers landed in Egypt on 4 or 5 June 1249 and began their campaign with the capture of the port of <a href="/wiki/Damietta" title="Damietta">Damietta</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Encarta-Crusades_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Encarta-Crusades-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETyerman2006787_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETyerman2006787-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> This attack caused some disruption in the Muslim <a href="/wiki/Ayyubid_dynasty" title="Ayyubid dynasty">Ayyubid</a> empire, especially as the current sultan, <a href="/wiki/As-Salih_Ayyub" title="As-Salih Ayyub">Al-Malik as-Salih Najm al-Din Ayyub</a>, was on his deathbed. However, the march of Europeans from Damietta toward <a href="/wiki/Cairo" title="Cairo">Cairo</a> through the <a href="/wiki/Nile_River_Delta" class="mw-redirect" title="Nile River Delta">Nile River Delta</a> went slowly. The seasonal rising of the Nile and the summer heat made it impossible for them to advance.<sup id="cite_ref-crawley_16-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-crawley-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> During this time, the Ayyubid sultan died, and the sultan's wife <a href="/wiki/Shajar_al-Durr" title="Shajar al-Durr">Shajar al-Durr</a> set in motion a shift in power that would make her Queen and eventually result in the rule of the Egyptian army of the <a href="/wiki/Mamluks" class="mw-redirect" title="Mamluks">Mamluks</a>.
</p><p>On 8 February 1250, Louis lost his army at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Fariskur_(1250)" title="Battle of Fariskur (1250)">Battle of Fariskur</a> and was captured by the Egyptians. His release was eventually negotiated in return for a ransom of 400,000 <i><a href="/wiki/Livre_tournois" title="Livre tournois">livres tournois</a></i> (roughly 80 million USD today) and the surrender of the city of Damietta.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETyerman2006796_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETyerman2006796-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Four_years_in_the_Kingdom_of_Jerusalem">Four years in the Kingdom of Jerusalem</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_IX_of_France&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Four years in the Kingdom of Jerusalem">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Upon his liberation from captivity in Egypt, Louis IX devoted four years to fortifying the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Jerusalem" title="Kingdom of Jerusalem">Kingdom of Jerusalem</a>, focusing his efforts in <a href="/wiki/Acre,_Israel" title="Acre, Israel">Acre</a>, <a href="/wiki/Caesarea,_Israel" title="Caesarea, Israel">Caesarea</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Jaffa" title="Jaffa">Jaffa</a>. He generously utilized his resources to aid the Crusaders in reconstructing their defenses<sup id="cite_ref-brehier_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-brehier-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> and actively engaged in diplomatic endeavors with the Islamic powers of <a href="/wiki/Syria" title="Syria">Syria</a> and <a href="/wiki/Egypt" title="Egypt">Egypt</a>. In spring 1254, Louis and his remaining forces made their return to France.<sup id="cite_ref-Encarta-Crusades_22-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Encarta-Crusades-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Louis maintained regular correspondence and envoy exchanges with the <a href="/wiki/Mongols" title="Mongols">Mongol</a> rulers of his era. During his first crusade in 1248, he received envoys from <a href="/wiki/Eljigidei" title="Eljigidei">Eljigidei</a>, the Mongol military leader stationed in <a href="/wiki/Armenia" title="Armenia">Armenia</a> and <a href="/wiki/Iran" title="Iran">Persia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJackson1980481-513_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson1980481-513-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> Eljigidei proposed that Louis should launch an offensive in Egypt while he targeted <a href="/wiki/Baghdad" title="Baghdad">Baghdad</a> to prevent the unification of the Muslim forces in Egypt and Syria. In response, Louis sent <a href="/wiki/Andr%C3%A9_de_Longjumeau" title="André de Longjumeau">André de Longjumeau</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Order" title="Dominican Order">Dominican</a> priest, as a delegate to the <a href="/wiki/Khagan" title="Khagan">Khangan</a> <a href="/wiki/G%C3%BCy%C3%BCk_Khan" title="Güyük Khan">Güyük Khan</a> (<span style="white-space:nowrap;"><abbr title="reigned">r.</abbr>&#8201;1246–1248</span>) in <a href="/wiki/Mongolia" title="Mongolia">Mongolia</a>. However, Güyük's death preceded the arrival of the emissary, and his widow and acting regent, <a href="/wiki/Oghul_Qaimish" title="Oghul Qaimish">Oghul Qaimish</a>, rejected the diplomatic proposition.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Louis sent another representative, the <a href="/wiki/Franciscans" title="Franciscans">Franciscan</a> missionary and explorer <a href="/wiki/William_of_Rubruck" title="William of Rubruck">William of Rubruck</a>, to the Mongol court. Rubruck visited the  Khagan <a href="/wiki/M%C3%B6ngke_Khan" title="Möngke Khan">Möngke</a> (<span style="white-space:nowrap;"><abbr title="reigned">r.</abbr>&#8201;1251–1259</span>) in Mongolia and spent several years there. In 1259, <a href="/wiki/Berke" title="Berke">Berke</a>, the leader of the <a href="/wiki/Golden_Horde" title="Golden Horde">Golden Horde</a>, demanded Louis's submission.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> In contrast, Mongol emperors Möngke and <a href="/wiki/Kublai_Khan" title="Kublai Khan">Khubilai</a>'s brother, the <a href="/wiki/Ilkhan_(title)" class="mw-redirect" title="Ilkhan (title)">Ilkhan</a> <a href="/wiki/Hulagu_Khan" title="Hulagu Khan">Hulegu</a>, sent a letter to the French king, soliciting his military aid; this letter, however, never reached France.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Later_reign_.281267.E2.80.931270.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Later_reign_(1267–1270)">Later reign (1267–1270)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_IX_of_France&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Later reign (1267–1270)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Eighth_Crusade_and_death">Eighth Crusade and death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_IX_of_France&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Eighth Crusade and death">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Mort_de_Louis_IX_le_Saint.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dc/Mort_de_Louis_IX_le_Saint.jpg/290px-Mort_de_Louis_IX_le_Saint.jpg" decoding="async" width="290" height="320" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dc/Mort_de_Louis_IX_le_Saint.jpg/435px-Mort_de_Louis_IX_le_Saint.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dc/Mort_de_Louis_IX_le_Saint.jpg/580px-Mort_de_Louis_IX_le_Saint.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1046" data-file-height="1154" /></a><figcaption>Death of Saint Louis: On 25 August 1270, Saint Louis dies in his tent, ornamented with royal symbols, near <a href="/wiki/Tunis" title="Tunis">Tunis</a>. Illuminated by <a href="/wiki/Jean_Fouquet" title="Jean Fouquet">Jean Fouquet</a>, <i><a href="/wiki/Grandes_Chroniques_de_France" title="Grandes Chroniques de France">Grandes Chroniques de France</a></i> (1455–1460)</figcaption></figure>
<p>In a parliament held at Paris, 24 March 1267, Louis and his three sons "took the cross." On hearing the reports of the missionaries, Louis resolved to land at <a href="/wiki/Tunis" title="Tunis">Tunis</a>, and he ordered his younger brother, Charles of Anjou, to join him there. The crusaders, among whom was the English prince <a href="/wiki/Edward_I_of_England" title="Edward I of England">Edward Longshanks</a>, landed at <a href="/wiki/Carthage" title="Carthage">Carthage</a> 17 July 1270, but disease broke out in the camp.<sup id="cite_ref-brehier_25-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-brehier-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Louis died at <a href="/wiki/Tunis" title="Tunis">Tunis</a> on 25 August 1270, in an epidemic of <a href="/wiki/Dysentery" title="Dysentery">dysentery</a> that swept through his army.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMagillAves1998606_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMagillAves1998606-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECrossLivingstone20051004_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrossLivingstone20051004-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELock2013183_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELock2013183-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> According to European custom, his body was subjected to the process known as <i><a href="/wiki/Mos_Teutonicus" title="Mos Teutonicus">mos Teutonicus</a></i> prior to his remains being returned to France.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWesterhof200879_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWesterhof200879-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> Louis was succeeded as King of France by his son, <a href="/wiki/Philip_III_of_France" title="Philip III of France">Philip III</a>.
</p><p>
Louis's younger brother, <a href="/wiki/Charles_I_of_Naples" class="mw-redirect" title="Charles I of Naples">Charles I of Naples</a>, preserved his heart and intestines, and conveyed them for burial in the <a href="/wiki/Monreale_Cathedral" title="Monreale Cathedral">Cathedral of Monreale</a> near <a href="/wiki/Palermo" title="Palermo">Palermo</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGaposchkin200828_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaposchkin200828-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup></p><figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Royal_MS_19_D_I,_fol._227r.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/3/3f/Royal_MS_19_D_I%2C_fol._227r.png/220px-Royal_MS_19_D_I%2C_fol._227r.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="166" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/3/3f/Royal_MS_19_D_I%2C_fol._227r.png/330px-Royal_MS_19_D_I%2C_fol._227r.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/3/3f/Royal_MS_19_D_I%2C_fol._227r.png/440px-Royal_MS_19_D_I%2C_fol._227r.png 2x" data-file-width="937" data-file-height="708" /></a><figcaption>Louis's body returning, from a copy of the crusade treatise <i><a href="/wiki/Directorium_ad_passagium" class="mw-redirect" title="Directorium ad passagium">Directorium ad passagium</a></i></figcaption></figure><p>Louis's bones were carried overland in a lengthy processional across Sicily, Italy, the Alps, and France, until they were interred in the royal necropolis at <a href="/wiki/Saint_Denis_Basilica" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint Denis Basilica">Saint-Denis</a> in May 1271<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGaposchkin200828–29_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaposchkin200828–29-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> Charles and Philip III later dispersed a number of <a href="/wiki/Relic" title="Relic">relics</a> to promote Louis's veneration.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGaposchkin200828–30,_76_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaposchkin200828–30,_76-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p><h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Children">Children</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_IX_of_France&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Children">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<ol><li>Blanche (12 July/4 December 1240 – 29 April 1244), died in infancy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERichard1983xxiv_7-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERichard1983xxiv-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Isabella_of_France,_Queen_of_Navarre" title="Isabella of France, Queen of Navarre">Isabella</a> (2 March 1241 – 28 January 1271), married <a href="/wiki/Theobald_II_of_Navarre" title="Theobald II of Navarre">Theobald II of Navarre</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJordan201725_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJordan201725-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Louis_of_France_(1244%E2%80%931260)" title="Louis of France (1244–1260)">Louis</a> (23 September 1243/24 February 1244 – 11 January/2 February 1260). Betrothed to <a href="/wiki/Berengaria_of_Castile,_Lady_of_Guadalajara" title="Berengaria of Castile, Lady of Guadalajara">Berengaria of Castile</a> in Paris on 20 August 1255.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJordan201725–26_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJordan201725–26-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Philip_III_of_France" title="Philip III of France">Philip III</a> (1 May 1245 – 5 October 1285), married firstly to <a href="/wiki/Isabella_of_Aragon,_Queen_of_France" title="Isabella of Aragon, Queen of France">Isabella of Aragon</a> in 1262 and secondly to <a href="/wiki/Marie_of_Brabant,_Queen_of_France" title="Marie of Brabant, Queen of France">Maria of Brabant</a> in 1274.</li>
<li>John (1246/1247 – 10 March 1248), died in infancy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERichard1983xxiv_7-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERichard1983xxiv-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/John_Tristan,_Count_of_Valois" title="John Tristan, Count of Valois">John Tristan</a> (8 April 1250 – 3 August 1270), Count of Valois, married <a href="/wiki/Yolande_II,_Countess_of_Nevers" title="Yolande II, Countess of Nevers">Yolande II, Countess of Nevers</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERichard1983xxiv_7-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERichard1983xxiv-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Peter_I,_Count_of_Alen%C3%A7on" title="Peter I, Count of Alençon">Peter</a> (1251 – 6/7 April 1284),<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERichard1983xxiv_7-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERichard1983xxiv-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> Count of Perche and Alençon, married <a href="/wiki/Joanne_of_Ch%C3%A2tillon" class="mw-redirect" title="Joanne of Châtillon">Joanne of Châtillon</a>.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Blanche_of_France,_Infanta_of_Castile" title="Blanche of France, Infanta of Castile">Blanche</a> (early 1253 – 17 June 1320), married <a href="/wiki/Ferdinand_de_la_Cerda,_Infante_of_Castile" class="mw-redirect" title="Ferdinand de la Cerda, Infante of Castile">Ferdinand de la Cerda, Infante of Castile</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERichard1983xxiv_7-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERichard1983xxiv-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Margaret_of_France,_Duchess_of_Brabant" title="Margaret of France, Duchess of Brabant">Margaret</a> (early 1255 – July 1271), married <a href="/wiki/John_I,_Duke_of_Brabant" title="John I, Duke of Brabant">John I, Duke of Brabant</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERichard1983xxiv_7-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERichard1983xxiv-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Robert,_Count_of_Clermont" title="Robert, Count of Clermont">Robert</a> (1256 – 7 February 1317), Count of Clermont,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERichard1983xxiv_7-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERichard1983xxiv-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> married <a href="/wiki/Beatrice_of_Burgundy,_Lady_of_Bourbon" title="Beatrice of Burgundy, Lady of Bourbon">Beatrice of Burgundy</a>. The French crown devolved upon his male-line descendant, <a href="/wiki/Henry_IV_of_France" title="Henry IV of France">Henry IV</a> (the first <a href="/wiki/House_of_Bourbon" title="House of Bourbon">Bourbon</a> king), when the legitimate male line of Philip III died out in 1589.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Agnes_of_France,_Duchess_of_Burgundy" title="Agnes of France, Duchess of Burgundy">Agnes</a> (1260 – 19/20 December 1327), married <a href="/wiki/Robert_II,_Duke_of_Burgundy" title="Robert II, Duke of Burgundy">Robert II, Duke of Burgundy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERichard1983xxiv_7-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERichard1983xxiv-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup></li></ol>
<p>Louis and Margaret's two children who died in infancy were first buried at the <a href="/wiki/Cistercians" title="Cistercians">Cistercian</a> <a href="/wiki/Royaumont_Abbey" title="Royaumont Abbey">abbey of Royaumont</a>. In 1820 they were transferred and reinterred to <a href="/wiki/Saint-Denis_Basilica" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint-Denis Basilica">Saint-Denis Basilica</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrown1990810_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown1990810-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Ancestry">Ancestry</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_IX_of_France&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Ancestry">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Veneration_as_a_saint">Veneration as a saint</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_IX_of_France&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Veneration as a saint">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"><table class="infobox vcard"><tbody><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-above n" style="background-color:gold;"><div class="honorific-prefix" style="display:inline;font-size: 77%; font-weight: normal;"><a href="/wiki/Saint" title="Saint">Saint</a></div><br /><div style="display:inline;" class="fn">Louis</div></th></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-image"><span class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Frameless"><a href="/wiki/File:San_Luis,_rey_de_Francia,_de_Francisco_Pacheco_(Museo_de_Bellas_Artes_de_Sevilla).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/95/San_Luis%2C_rey_de_Francia%2C_de_Francisco_Pacheco_%28Museo_de_Bellas_Artes_de_Sevilla%29.jpg/220px-San_Luis%2C_rey_de_Francia%2C_de_Francisco_Pacheco_%28Museo_de_Bellas_Artes_de_Sevilla%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="439" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/9/95/San_Luis%2C_rey_de_Francia%2C_de_Francisco_Pacheco_%28Museo_de_Bellas_Artes_de_Sevilla%29.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="310" data-file-height="618" /></a></span><div class="infobox-caption"><i>San Luis, Rey de Francia</i> (<a href="/wiki/English_language" title="English language">English</a>: Saint Louis, King of France) by <a href="/wiki/Francisco_Pacheco" title="Francisco Pacheco">Francisco Pacheco</a></div></td></tr><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header" style="background-color:gold;">King of France <br /> <a href="/wiki/Confessor" title="Confessor">Confessor</a></th></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Venerated&#160;in</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a><br /><a href="/wiki/Anglican_Communion" title="Anglican Communion">Anglican Communion</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Canonization" title="Canonization">Canonized</a></th><td class="infobox-data">11 July 1297, <a href="/wiki/Rome" title="Rome">Rome</a>, <a href="/wiki/Papal_States" title="Papal States">Papal States</a> by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Boniface_VIII" title="Pope Boniface VIII">Pope Boniface VIII</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints" title="Calendar of saints">Feast</a></th><td class="infobox-data">25 August</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Saint_symbolism" title="Saint symbolism">Attributes</a></th><td class="infobox-data">The <a href="/wiki/Crown_of_Thorns" class="mw-redirect" title="Crown of Thorns">Crown of Thorns</a>, <a href="/wiki/Crown" title="Crown">crown</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sceptre" title="Sceptre">sceptre</a>, <i><a href="/wiki/Globus_cruciger" title="Globus cruciger">globus cruciger</a></i>, <a href="/wiki/Sword" title="Sword">sword</a>, <i><a href="/wiki/Fleur-de-lis" title="Fleur-de-lis">fleur-de-lis</a></i>, <a href="/wiki/Mantle_(royal_garment)" title="Mantle (royal garment)">mantle</a>, and the other parts of the <a href="/wiki/French_regalia" class="mw-redirect" title="French regalia">French regalia</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">Patronage</a></th><td class="infobox-data"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1126788409"><div class="plainlist"><ul><li><a href="/wiki/France" title="France">France</a></li><li><a href="/wiki/Third_Order_of_Saint_Francis" title="Third Order of Saint Francis">Third Order of Saint Francis</a></li><li>The <a href="/wiki/French_Monarchy" class="mw-redirect" title="French Monarchy">French Monarchy</a></li><li><a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_New_Orleans" title="Roman Catholic Archdiocese of New Orleans">Archdiocese of New Orleans</a></li><li><a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Diocese_of_Port-Louis" title="Roman Catholic Diocese of Port-Louis">Diocese of Port-Louis</a></li><li><a href="/wiki/Barber" title="Barber">barbers</a></li><li><a href="/wiki/Groom" class="mw-redirect" title="Groom">grooms</a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><a href="/wiki/Pope_Boniface_VIII" title="Pope Boniface VIII">Pope Boniface VIII</a> proclaimed the canonization of Louis in 1297;<sup id="cite_ref-ODS_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODS-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> he is the only French king to be declared a <a href="/wiki/Saint" title="Saint">saint</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> Louis IX is often considered the model of the ideal Christian monarch.<sup id="cite_ref-ODS_40-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODS-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Named in his honour, the <a href="/wiki/Sisters_of_Charity_of_St._Louis" title="Sisters of Charity of St. Louis">Sisters of Charity of St. Louis</a> is a Roman Catholic <a href="/wiki/Religious_order_(Catholic)" title="Religious order (Catholic)">religious order</a> founded in <a href="/wiki/Vannes" title="Vannes">Vannes</a>, France, in 1803.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> A similar order, the Sisters of St Louis, was founded in <a href="/wiki/Juilly,_Seine-et-Marne" title="Juilly, Seine-et-Marne">Juilly</a> in 1842.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He is honoured as co-patron of the <a href="/wiki/Third_Order_of_St._Francis" class="mw-redirect" title="Third Order of St. Francis">Third Order of St. Francis</a>, which claims him as a member of the Order. When he became king, over a hundred poor people were served meals in his house on ordinary days. Often the king served these guests himself. His acts of charity, coupled with his devout religious practices, gave rise to the legend that he joined the Third Order of St. Francis, though it is unlikely that he ever actually joined the order.<sup id="cite_ref-archstl_8-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-archstl-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Catholic Church and <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Episcopal_Church)" title="Calendar of saints (Episcopal Church)">Episcopal Church</a> honor him with a feast day on 25 August.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Things_named_after_Saint_Louis">Things named after Saint Louis</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_IX_of_France&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Things named after Saint Louis">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<ul><li>The French royal <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Saint_Louis" title="Order of Saint Louis">Order of Saint Louis</a> (1693–1790 and 1814–1830)<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Places">Places</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_IX_of_France&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Places">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Many countries in which French speakers and Catholicism were prevalent named places after King Louis:
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/San_Luis_Province" title="San Luis Province">San Luis Province</a> in Argentina<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/San_Luis_Potos%C3%AD" title="San Luis Potosí">San Luis Potosí</a> in Mexico<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Multiple locations in the United States
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/St._Louis" title="St. Louis">St. Louis, Missouri</a>, named by French colonists<sup id="cite_ref-CDWPN_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CDWPN-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup></li></ul></li>
<li>Multiple locations in France<sup id="cite_ref-CDWPN_50-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CDWPN-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/%C3%8Ele_Saint-Louis" title="Île Saint-Louis">Île Saint-Louis</a>, an island in the river <a href="/wiki/Seine" title="Seine">Seine</a>, Paris<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Saint-Louis,_New_Caledonia" title="Saint-Louis, New Caledonia">Saint-Louis, New Caledonia</a><sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (February 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup></li></ul></li>
<li>Multiple locations in Canada<sup id="cite_ref-CDWPN_50-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CDWPN-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Saint-Louis,_Senegal" title="Saint-Louis, Senegal">Saint-Louis, Senegal</a><sup id="cite_ref-CDWPN_50-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CDWPN-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/S%C3%A3o_Lu%C3%ADs,_Maranh%C3%A3o" title="São Luís, Maranhão">São Luís, Maranhão</a> in Brazil<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>The Philippines
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/San_Luis,_Aurora" title="San Luis, Aurora">San Luis, Aurora</a><sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/San_Luis,_Batangas" title="San Luis, Batangas">San Luis, Batangas</a><sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup></li></ul></li></ul>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Buildings">Buildings</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_IX_of_France&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Buildings">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<ul><li>France
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/H%C3%B4pital_Saint-Louis" title="Hôpital Saint-Louis">Hôpital Saint-Louis</a>, hospital in the 10th arrondissement of Paris<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>The <a href="/wiki/Versailles_Cathedral" title="Versailles Cathedral">Cathédrale Saint-Louis de Versailles</a> in <a href="/wiki/Versailles,_Yvelines" title="Versailles, Yvelines">Versailles</a><sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup></li></ul></li>
<li>United States
<ul><li>The <a href="/wiki/Basilica_of_St._Louis,_King_of_France" title="Basilica of St. Louis, King of France">Basilica of St. Louis, King of France</a>,  completed in 1834 in St. Louis, Missouri<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>The <a href="/wiki/Cathedral_Basilica_of_Saint_Louis_(St._Louis)" title="Cathedral Basilica of Saint Louis (St. Louis)">Cathedral Basilica of St. Louis</a>,  completed in 1914 in St. Louis, Missouri <sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>The <a href="/wiki/St._Louis_Cathedral_(New_Orleans)" title="St. Louis Cathedral (New Orleans)">St. Louis Cathedral in New Orleans</a><sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>The St. Louis King of France Catholic Church and School, in Metairie, Louisiana  <sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Saint Louis Catholic High School, in Lake Charles, Louisiana</li>
<li>Saint Louis King of France Catholic Church and School, in Austin, Texas</li>
<li>Saint Louis Catholic Church, in Waco, Texas</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Mission_San_Luis_Rey_de_Francia" title="Mission San Luis Rey de Francia">Mission San Luis Rey de Francia</a>, Oceanside, California, founded 12 June 1798</li>
<li>San Luis Rey Mission, Chamberino, New Mexico</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/St._Louis_Roman_Catholic_Church" title="St. Louis Roman Catholic Church">St. Louis Roman Catholic Church</a> in Buffalo, New York<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> (Mother Church of the Roman Catholic Diocese of Buffalo)<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup></li></ul></li>
<li>The <a href="/wiki/San_Luigi_dei_Francesi" title="San Luigi dei Francesi">national church of France</a> in Rome: San Luigi dei Francesi in Italian, or Saint Louis of France in English<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>The <a href="/wiki/Cathedral_of_St_Louis_(Plovdiv)" title="Cathedral of St Louis (Plovdiv)">Cathedral of St Louis</a> in Plovdiv, Bulgaria<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>The <a href="/wiki/Acropolium_of_Carthage" title="Acropolium of Carthage">Cathedral of St Louis</a> in Carthage, Tunisia, so named because Louis IX died at that approximate location in 1270<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>The <a href="/wiki/List_of_churches_in_Moscow#Centre" title="List of churches in Moscow">Church of St Louis</a> in Moscow, Russia<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>India
<ul><li>Rue Saint Louis of <a href="/wiki/Pondicherry_(city)" class="mw-redirect" title="Pondicherry (city)">Pondicherry</a><sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>St. Louis Church, Dahisar East, Mumbai. (<a rel="nofollow" class="external free" href="https://stlouischurchdahisar.in/">https://stlouischurchdahisar.in/</a>)</li></ul></li>
<li>The Convent of Saint Louis and Catholic High School in Carrickmacross, Ireland.</li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Notable_portraits">Notable portraits</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_IX_of_France&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Notable portraits">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<ul><li>United States
<ul><li>A bas-relief of St. Louis is one of the carved portraits of historic lawmakers that adorn <a href="/wiki/United_States_Capitol#House_Chamber" title="United States Capitol">the chamber</a> of the <a href="/wiki/United_States_House_of_Representatives" title="United States House of Representatives">United States House of Representatives</a>.</li>
<li>Saint Louis is also portrayed on a <a href="/wiki/Frieze" title="Frieze">frieze</a> depicting a timeline of important lawgivers throughout world history, on the North Wall of the <a href="/wiki/Courtroom" title="Courtroom">Courtroom</a> at the <a href="/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_the_United_States" title="Supreme Court of the United States">Supreme Court of the United States</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>A statue of St. Louis by the sculptor John Donoghue stands on the roofline of the <a href="/wiki/New_York_State_Appellate_Division" class="mw-redirect" title="New York State Appellate Division">New York State Appellate Division</a> Court at 27 Madison Avenue in New York City.</li>
<li>The <a href="/wiki/Apotheosis_of_St._Louis" title="Apotheosis of St. Louis">Apotheosis of St. Louis</a> is an equestrian statue of the saint, by <a href="/wiki/Charles_Henry_Niehaus" title="Charles Henry Niehaus">Charles Henry Niehaus</a>, that stands in front of the <a href="/wiki/Saint_Louis_Art_Museum" title="Saint Louis Art Museum">Saint Louis Art Museum</a> in Forest Park.</li>
<li>A heroic portrait by <a href="/wiki/Charles_de_Steuben" title="Charles de Steuben">Baron Charles de Steuben</a> hangs in the <a href="/wiki/Basilica_of_the_National_Shrine_of_the_Assumption_of_the_Blessed_Virgin_Mary_(Baltimore)" class="mw-redirect" title="Basilica of the National Shrine of the Assumption of the Blessed Virgin Mary (Baltimore)">Basilica of the National Shrine of the Assumption of the Blessed Virgin Mary</a> in <a href="/wiki/Baltimore" title="Baltimore">Baltimore</a>.  An 1821 gift of <a href="/wiki/Louis_XVIII_of_France" class="mw-redirect" title="Louis XVIII of France">King Louis XVIII of France</a>, it depicts St. Louis burying his plague-stricken troops before the siege of <a href="/wiki/Tunis" title="Tunis">Tunis</a> at the beginning of the <a href="/wiki/Eighth_Crusade" title="Eighth Crusade">Eighth Crusade</a> in 1270.</li></ul></li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="In_fiction">In fiction</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_IX_of_France&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: In fiction">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<ul><li>Davis, William Stearns, <i>"Falaise of the Blessed Voice"</i> aka <i>"The White Queen"</i>. New York, NY: Macmillan, 1904</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Peter_Berling" title="Peter Berling">Peter Berling</a>, <i><a href="/wiki/The_Children_of_the_Grail" title="The Children of the Grail">The Children of the Grail</a></i></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Jules_Verne" title="Jules Verne">Jules Verne</a>, "To the Sun?/Off on a Comet!" A comet takes several bits of the Earth away when it grazes the Earth. Some people, taken up at the same time, find the Tomb of Saint Louis is one of the bits, as they explore the comet.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Adam_Gidwitz" title="Adam Gidwitz">Adam Gidwitz</a>, <i>The Inquisitor's Tale</i></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Dante_Alighieri" title="Dante Alighieri">Dante Alighieri</a>, <i><a href="/wiki/Divine_Comedy" title="Divine Comedy">Divina Commedia</a>.</i> It is likely that Dante hides the figure of the Saint King behind the Veltro, the Messo di Dio, the Veglio di Creta and the "515", which is a duplicate of the Messo. This is a trinitarian representation to oppose to the analogous representation of his nephew <a href="/wiki/Philip_IV_of_France" title="Philip IV of France">Philip IV the Fair</a>, as the <a href="/wiki/The_Beast_(Revelation)" title="The Beast (Revelation)">Beast from the Sea</a>. The idea came to Dante from the transposition of the Revelation of St. John in the history, studied from the abbot and theologian <a href="/wiki/Joachim_of_Fiore" title="Joachim of Fiore">Joachim of Fiore</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Theodore de Bainville, poem, "La Ballade des Pendus (Le Verger du Roi Louis)"; musicalized by Georges Brassens.</li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Music">Music</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Louis_IX_of_France&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Music">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<ul><li><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arnaud_du_Prat&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Arnaud du Prat (page does not exist)">Arnaud du Prat</a>, Paris canon; Rhymed, chanted office for St. Louis, 1290, Sens Bib. Mun. MS6, and elsewhere.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Marc-Antoine_Charpentier" title="Marc-Antoine Charpentier">Marc-Antoine Charpentier</a>, <i>Motet for Saint Louis,</i> H.320, for 1 voice, 2 treble instruments (?) and continuo 1675.</li>
<li>Marc-Antoine Charpentier<i>,</i> Motet <i>In honorem santi Ludovici Regis Galliae canticum tribus vocibus cum symphonia,</i> H.323, for 3 voices, 2 treble instruments and continuo (1678&#160;?)</li>
<li>Marc-Antoine Charpentier<i>,</i> Motet <i>In honorem Sancti Ludovici regis Galliae,</i> H.332, for 3 voices, 2 treble instruments and continuo 1683)</li>
<li>Marc-Antoine Charpentier, Motet <i>In honorem Sancti Ludovici regis Galliae canticum,</i> H.365 &amp; H.365 a, for soloists, chorus, woodwinds, strings and continuo (1690)</li>
<li>Marc-Antoine Charpentier, Motet <i>In honorem Sancti Ludovici regis Galliae,</i> H.418, for soloists, chorus, 2 flutes, 2 violins and continuo (1692–93)</li></ul>
